Task 24

Dataset: SF110 — Project: guava — Class: Monitor

Class summary

``` 1. Overall Purpose: The `Monitor` class from the `com.google.common.util.concurrent` package provides a flexible and robust mechanism for managing thread synchronization and coordination via conditions. It builds upon `ReentrantLock` to offer more sophisticated concurrency control, primarily by allowing threads to wait for certain conditions (encapsulated by `Guard` objects) to be met before proceeding, making it suitable for scenarios where complex thread interactions are managed. 2. Key Elements Identification: - Main Methods: - `enter()`, `enterInterruptibly()`, `tryEnter()`: These methods acquire the lock, with variations to handle interruptions and attempt non-blocking acquisition. - `enter(long time, TimeUnit unit)`, `enterInterruptibly(long time, TimeUnit unit)`: These methods attempt to acquire the lock within a specified timeframe, with or without handling interruptions. - `enterWhen(Guard guard)`, `enterWhenUninterruptibly(Guard guard)`: These methods acquire the lock and wait for the associated `Guard` condition to be satisfied, with one handling interruptions. - `enterWhen(Guard guard, long time, TimeUnit unit)`, `enterWhenUninterruptibly(Guard guard, long time, TimeUnit unit)`: These methods are time-bound versions of `enterWhen`, waiting for a condition to be satisfied within a specified time. - `enterIf(Guard guard)`, `enterIfInterruptibly(Guard guard)`: Acquire the lock only if the `Guard` condition is satisfied upon entry, with an interruptible variant. - `leave()`: Releases the lock and signals the next waiting thread if any. - `waitFor(Guard guard)`, `waitForUninterruptibly(Guard guard)`: Waits for the `Guard` condition to be satisfied while holding the lock, with one supporting interruptions. - `await(Guard guard, boolean signalBeforeWaiting)`, `awaitUninterruptibly(Guard guard, boolean signalBeforeWaiting)`: Helper methods for waiting on a condition, with or without handling interruptions. - `awaitNanos(Guard guard, long nanos, boolean signalBeforeWaiting)`: Waits for a condition to be satisfied within a given timeout. - Important Variables: - `fair`: A boolean indicating if the lock should use a fair ordering policy. - `lock`: A `ReentrantLock` used to enforce thread synchronization. - `activeGuards`: A linked list of active `Guard` objects, managed by the lock to track waiting conditions. 3. Method Relationships: - Lock Acquisition Methods: `enter`, `enterInterruptibly`, `tryEnter`, and their timed variants handle basic lock acquisition with different interruption and timeout strategies. - Condition Waiting Methods: `enterWhen`, `enterWhenUninterruptibly`, `waitFor`, and their timed variants manage waiting based on `Guard` conditions. - Helper Methods: `await`, `awaitUninterruptibly`, and `awaitNanos` are used internally to manage condition waiting logic in a synchronized manner. 4. Summarize by Sections: - Lock Management: The class provides methods to acquire and release a lock (`enter`, `leave`), supporting various strategies for managing thread access and interruptions. - Condition Synchronization: Methods like `enterWhen`, `waitFor`, and their variants allow threads to wait for specific conditions, encapsulated by `Guard` objects, to be met, enhancing control over thread execution flow. - Guard Management: The `Guard` abstract class defines conditions that threads can wait upon, offering a mechanism to encapsulate complex logic for determining when a thread should proceed. The `Monitor` class effectively combines lock acquisition with condition-based waiting, providing a comprehensive solution for thread synchronization in concurrent applications. ```

Task 25

Dataset: SF110 — Project: guava — Class: Predicates

Class summary

``` 1. Overall Purpose: The `Predicates` class in the `com.google.common.base` package provides a collection of static utility methods for creating and combining `Predicate` instances. These predicates are primarily used for functional-style programming, enabling developers to define complex logical conditions in a concise manner. 2. Key Elements Identification: - Main methods: - `alwaysTrue()`, `alwaysFalse()`, `isNull()`, `notNull()`: These methods return simple predicates that test if a condition is always true, always false, if an object is null, or not null, respectively. - `not(Predicate<T>)`: Returns a predicate that negates the result of the given predicate. - `and(...)`: Overloaded methods that return a predicate representing the logical AND of multiple predicates. - `or(...)`: Overloaded methods that create a predicate representing the logical OR of several predicates. - `equalTo(T)`: Returns a predicate that checks if an object equals the specified target. - `instanceOf(Class<?>)`, `assignableFrom(Class<?>)`: Return predicates that check if an object is an instance of or assignable from a given class. - `in(Collection<? extends T>)`: Returns a predicate that checks if an object is in a given collection. - `compose(Predicate<B>, Function<A, ? extends B>)`: Returns a predicate that applies a function to its input and then applies another predicate to the result. - `containsPattern(String)`, `contains(Pattern)`: Return predicates that check if a CharSequence contains a specified regex pattern. - Important variables: - `COMMA_JOINER`: A static `Joiner` instance used to concatenate components with a comma, primarily for generating string representations of compound predicates. - `ObjectPredicate`: An enum that provides predefined predicates for common conditions like `ALWAYS_TRUE`, `ALWAYS_FALSE`, `IS_NULL`, and `NOT_NULL`. 3. Method Relationships: The `and` and `or` methods are overloaded to accept different types of input (single predicates, arrays, or collections), providing flexibility in how compound predicates are constructed. The helper methods `asList`, `defensiveCopy`, and `withNarrowedType` are used internally to manage collections of predicates, ensuring immutability and type safety. 4. Summarize by Sections: - Basic Predicates (`alwaysTrue`, `alwaysFalse`, `isNull`, `notNull`): These methods provide basic predicates for fundamental logical checks, covering common scenarios that require simple true/false evaluations. - Logical Combinators (`not`, `and`, `or`): These methods support combining predicates in complex logical expressions, allowing for the creation of composite conditions that evaluate multiple predicates using logical operations like NOT, AND, and OR. - Object and Class Predicates (`equalTo`, `instanceOf`, `assignableFrom`, `in`): These methods return predicates that perform object comparison, type-checking, and collection membership tests, useful for filtering and matching operations. - Pattern Matching (`containsPattern`, `contains`): These predicates are used for matching text against regular expressions, facilitating pattern recognition in strings and character sequences. - Helper Methods and Internal Classes: Internal classes such as `NotPredicate`, `AndPredicate`, `OrPredicate`, etc., provide the implementation for the logic defined in the main methods. Helper methods like `asList` and `defensiveCopy` ensure safe and consistent handling of predicate lists. ```

    @Test(timeout = 4000)
    public void testNotPredicateWithAlwaysTrue() throws Throwable {
        // Given: An ALWAYS_TRUE ObjectPredicate
        Predicates.ObjectPredicate alwaysTruePredicate = Predicates.ObjectPredicate.ALWAYS_TRUE;

        // When: Creating a not predicate and using it with CharMatcher
        Predicate<Object> notPredicate = Predicates.not(alwaysTruePredicate);
        CharMatcher charMatcher = CharMatcher.forPredicate(notPredicate);
        char[] charArray = new char[10];
        CharBuffer charBuffer = CharBuffer.wrap(charArray);
        String result = charMatcher.trimAndCollapseFrom(charBuffer, '\u0000');

        // Then: The result should be a string of null characters
        assertEquals("Result should be a string of null characters", "\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000\u0000", result);
    }

    @Test(timeout = 4000)
    public void testComposePredicateWithIdentityConverter() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A NOT_NULL ObjectPredicate
        Predicates.ObjectPredicate notNullPredicate = Predicates.ObjectPredicate.NOT_NULL;

        // When: Creating an or predicate and composing it with an identity converter
        Predicate<Predicates.ObjectPredicate> orPredicate = Predicates.or(notNullPredicate, notNullPredicate);
        Converter<Predicates.ObjectPredicate, Predicates.ObjectPredicate> identityConverter = Converter.identity();
        Predicate<Predicates.ObjectPredicate> composedPredicate = Predicates.compose(orPredicate, identityConverter);

        // Then: The composed predicate should not be equal to the original or predicate
        assertFalse("Composed predicate should not be equal to original or predicate", composedPredicate.equals(orPredicate));
    }

Task 26

Dataset: SF110 — Project: guava — Class: Suppliers

Class summary

``` 1. Overall Purpose: The `Suppliers` class is a utility class within the Google Guava library that provides various static methods to create and manipulate `Supplier` instances. These suppliers are used to provide values on demand, and the class offers several ways to manage the computation and caching of these values, making it useful for scenarios where lazy computation or caching is required. 2. Key Elements Identification: - Main methods: - `compose(final Function<? super F, T> function, final Supplier<F> supplier)`: Returns a supplier that applies a given function to the result from another supplier. Takes a function and a supplier as inputs and returns a composed supplier. - `memoize(final Supplier<T> delegate)`: Wraps a supplier to cache the value it provides, ensuring it's computed only once. Takes a supplier and returns a memoized supplier. - `memoizeWithExpiration(final Supplier<T> delegate, final long duration, final TimeUnit unit)`: Similar to `memoize`, but with a caching duration after which the value is recomputed. Takes a supplier, a duration, and a time unit, returning an expiring memoized supplier. - `ofInstance(@Nullable final T instance)`: Creates a supplier that always returns the same instance provided. Takes an instance and returns a supplier. - `synchronizedSupplier(final Supplier<T> delegate)`: Wraps a supplier to make its `get` method thread-safe. Takes a supplier and returns a synchronized supplier. - `supplierFunction()`: Provides a function that retrieves a value from a supplier. Returns a function that operates on suppliers. - Important variables: - `SupplierComposition`: Inner class representing a supplier that applies a function to another supplier's value. - `MemoizingSupplier`: Inner class that caches the value from its delegate supplier. - `ExpiringMemoizingSupplier`: Inner class that caches the value with an expiration time. - `SupplierOfInstance`: Inner class that returns a constant instance. - `ThreadSafeSupplier`: Inner class ensuring thread-safe access to a supplier. - `SupplierFunctionImpl`: Enum implementation of a function that retrieves values from suppliers. 3. Method Relationships: The methods `memoize`, `memoizeWithExpiration`, and `synchronizedSupplier` all extend the functionality of a given supplier by adding caching or synchronization capabilities. The `compose` method is related by allowing transformation of the supplier's output using a function. 4. Summarize by Sections: - Supplier Composition: The `compose` method and `SupplierComposition` class work together to create suppliers that apply a given function to transform the output of another supplier. This pattern is useful for chaining operations in a functional programming style. - Memoization: The `memoize` and `memoizeWithExpiration` methods, along with their respective classes, provide mechanisms to cache the results of supplier computations, reducing redundant computations by storing the result for future use, with `memoizeWithExpiration` adding a time-based invalidation. - Instance Suppliers: The `ofInstance` method and `SupplierOfInstance` class create suppliers that always return the same instance, which is useful for constant or precomputed values that don't require additional computation. - Thread Safety: The `synchronizedSupplier` method and `ThreadSafeSupplier` class wrap a supplier to ensure that its `get` method is thread-safe, preventing concurrent access issues when multiple threads may access the supplier. - Supplier Function: The `supplierFunction` method provides a lightweight function interface to retrieve values from suppliers, which can be integrated seamlessly into other functional programming constructs. ```

Task 27

Dataset: SF110 — Project: javaml — Class: Fold

Class summary

``` 1. Overall Purpose: The `Fold` class is part of a larger data manipulation framework, likely used in machine learning contexts. Its primary function is to represent a subset (or fold) of a dataset, allowing access to specific data points based on an index array. This is useful for operations such as cross-validation in machine learning, where data is divided into training and testing folds. 2. Key Elements Identification: - Main methods: - `Fold(Dataset parent, int[] indices)`: Constructor that initializes the fold with a reference to the parent dataset and an array of indices indicating which instances are part of this fold. - `instance(int index)`: Returns the instance from the parent dataset at the specified index in the fold, allowing access to specific data points. - `get(int index)`: Delegates to `instance(int index)`, serving as an alias for retrieving instances. - `size()`: Returns the number of instances in the fold, corresponding to the length of the indices array. - `toArray()`: Converts the fold into an array of instances, iterating over the indices to retrieve each instance. - `toArray(T[] a)`: Similar to `toArray()`, but populates the provided array with instances. - `classes()`, `noAttributes()`, `classIndex(Object clazz)`, `classValue(int index)`: Proxy methods that delegate to the parent dataset for class-related information, maintaining consistency with the parent dataset. - `copy()`: Creates a deep copy of the fold by copying each instance into a new dataset, ensuring that the fold can be independently manipulated if needed. - Important variables: - `int[] indices`: An array storing indices of instances in the parent dataset that are part of the fold, crucial for indexing operations within the fold. - `Dataset parent`: A reference to the parent dataset, enabling access to the full dataset's methods and data. 3. Method Relationships: - `instance(int index)` and `get(int index)` are directly related, with `get` acting as a straightforward alias for `instance` to provide flexibility in method calls. - `toArray()` and `toArray(T[] a)` work together to provide flexibility in how the fold's data is output, allowing for conversion to standard Java arrays. - The class-related proxy methods (`classes()`, `noAttributes()`, `classIndex(Object clazz)`, `classValue(int index)`) collectively maintain logical consistency with the parent dataset, ensuring that the fold inherits characteristics from its parent. 4. Summarize by Sections: - Constructor and Initialization: The constructor initializes the fold with specific indices from a parent dataset, effectively creating a view of the data that is defined by these indices. This setup is crucial for performing operations like cross-validation. - Data Access Methods: Methods such as `instance`, `get`, and `size` provide direct access to the data within the fold, enabling users to retrieve specific instances and determine the number of instances. - Conversion Methods: The `toArray()` and its variant allow for exporting the fold's data into array formats, facilitating integration with other systems or processing routines. - Proxy and Copy Methods: Delegate methods to the parent dataset ensure the fold inherits dataset characteristics, while `copy()` allows for creating independent copies of the fold, highlighting flexibility and reusability. - FoldIterator: This inner class provides iteration capabilities over the fold, implementing the `ListIterator` interface to enable forward and backward traversal of the dataset's instances, while prohibiting modification to maintain fold integrity. ```

Version B

private static final int TIMEOUT = 4000;
    private static final int EMPTY_ARRAY_SIZE = 0;
    private static final int SINGLE_ELEMENT_ARRAY_SIZE = 1;
    private static final int MULTI_ELEMENT_ARRAY_SIZE = 8;

    @Test(timeout = TIMEOUT)
    public void testIteratorNotNull() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A Fold with a DefaultDataset and an array of indices
        DefaultDataset dataset = new DefaultDataset();
        int[] indices = new int[MULTI_ELEMENT_ARRAY_SIZE];
        Fold fold = new Fold(dataset, indices);

        // When: Retrieving the iterator
        Iterator<Instance> iterator = fold.iterator();

        // Then: The iterator should not be null
        assertNotNull("Iterator should not be null", iterator);
    }

    @Test(timeout = TIMEOUT)
    public void testInstanceAccessThrowsException() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A Fold with a DefaultDataset and an array of indices
        DefaultDataset dataset = new DefaultDataset();
        int[] indices = new int[5];
        Fold fold = new Fold(dataset, indices);

        // When: Accessing an instance at an invalid index
        // Then: Expect an ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException
        try {
            fold.instance(0);
            fail("Expecting exception: ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException");
        } catch (ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException e) {
            verifyException("java.util.Vector", e);
        }
    }

    @Test(timeout = TIMEOUT)
    public void testCopyFold() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A Fold with a DefaultDataset containing a DenseInstance
        DefaultDataset dataset = new DefaultDataset();
        double[] values = new double[5];
        DenseInstance instance = new DenseInstance(values);
        dataset.add(instance);
        int[] indices = new int[SINGLE_ELEMENT_ARRAY_SIZE];
        Fold fold = new Fold(dataset, indices);

        // When: Copying the fold
        Dataset copiedDataset = fold.copy();

        // Then: The copied dataset should have the same size as the fold
        assertEquals("Copied dataset size should match fold size", SINGLE_ELEMENT_ARRAY_SIZE, copiedDataset.size());
    }

    @Test(timeout = TIMEOUT)
    public void testToArrayWithPredefinedArray() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A Fold with a DefaultDataset containing a SparseInstance
        DefaultDataset dataset = new DefaultDataset();
        double[] values = new double[1];
        SparseInstance instance = new SparseInstance(values, 1847.67361);
        dataset.add(instance);
        int[] indices = new int[SINGLE_ELEMENT_ARRAY_SIZE];
        Fold fold = new Fold(dataset, indices);
        Object[] predefinedArray = new Object[6];

        // When: Converting the fold to an array using a predefined array
        Object[] resultArray = fold.toArray(predefinedArray);

        // Then: The result array should be the same as the predefined array
        assertSame("Result array should be the same as predefined array", predefinedArray, resultArray);
    }

    @Test(timeout = TIMEOUT)
    public void testToArrayThrowsNullPointerException() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A Fold with a null Dataset
        int[] indices = new int[2];
        Fold fold = new Fold(null, indices);

        // When: Converting the fold to an array
        // Then: Expect a NullPointerException
        try {
            fold.toArray();
            fail("Expecting exception: NullPointerException");
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            verifyException("net.sf.javaml.core.Fold", e);
        }
    }

    @Test(timeout = TIMEOUT)
    public void testToArrayWithEmptyFold() throws Throwable {
        // Given: A Fold with an empty DefaultDataset
        DefaultDataset dataset = new DefaultDataset();
        int[] indices = new int[EMPTY_ARRAY_SIZE];
        Fold fold = new Fold(dataset, indices);

        // When: Converting the fold to an array
        Object[] resultArray = fold.toArray();

        // Then: The result array should be empty
        assertEquals("Result array should be empty", EMPTY_ARRAY_SIZE, resultArray.length);
    }

Task 28

Dataset: SF110 — Project: scribe — Class: Verifier

Class summary

``` 1. Overall Purpose: The `Verifier` class is designed to encapsulate a verification string, typically used in OAuth authentication flows to confirm the identity of the requester. This class ensures that a valid verifier string is provided and can be retrieved when needed. 2. Key Elements Identification: - Main methods: - `Verifier(final String value)`: This constructor initializes a `Verifier` object with a given string, ensuring that the string is not null. It uses a utility function to check this precondition, signaling its importance to the proper functioning of the class. - `getValue()`: This method returns the verifier string stored within the object. It has no input parameters and returns a `String`. - Important variables: - `value`: This is a private, final string that holds the verifier's value. Its immutability (being `final`) ensures the integrity and consistency of the verifier once it is set during object construction. 3. Method Relationships: The constructor and `getValue` method are closely related, as they form the basic lifecycle of the `Verifier` object. The constructor sets the value, and `getValue` provides access to this value. They collectively ensure that a valid verifier is used and can be accessed while maintaining immutability. 4. Summarize by Sections: - Constructor Section: The constructor of the `Verifier` class is critical for initializing the object with a non-null verifier string. It uses a precondition check to guarantee that the object is always in a valid state. - Accessor Method Section: The `getValue` method provides a simple mechanism to retrieve the verifier string from the `Verifier` object. This method ensures that the encapsulated value is accessible while maintaining the class's immutability. ```
